ParlaMint-GR_2019-10-23-S1-commons.u20	Thank you, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, with the draft 'Investing in Greece' bill we are discussing today, the government is attempting to measure itself with the size of obstacles to entrepreneurship and distortions of the investment environment in our country. These are distortions and obstacles that are large in number and wide in scope. They run a wide range of our regulatory framework. They largely define the behaviour of the public administration and shape the attitude and culture of entrepreneurship and investment in the country. That is why the government has designed, presented and implemented a comprehensive intervention, with provisions covering almost all the ministries' responsibilities. Because only in this way will we be able to overcome the weaknesses that have been left in a deep past and the poor legalisation and the fragmentation which are deterrent to attract investment. And it is this multi-brand plan, ladies and gentlemen, with the many small reforms it brings, which will create a different picture and create major changes in the entrepreneurial environment, which will allow us to create a development framework that encourages and does not discourage, which will promote and does not block, which will highlight and does not compromise entrepreneurship and investment. It is a great need for these changes, ladies and gentlemen, if we consider three things: basic sizes of the economy, the country's classification of international indicators of competitiveness and the reality of those who are involved in investment and business in the country. In particular, investment in Greece, ladies and gentlemen, in the last decade, has literally collapsed. Since the 60 billion in 2007, foreign direct investment in the country has fallen to 20 billion in 2018, and today, foreign direct investment in the country remains at one of the lowest levels of the European Union. The rate of unemployment in Greece is still one of the highest in the European Union, of the 17.2%, with an average of the European Union of 6.3%. In July 2019, our trade balance deficit was close to 2 billion. 6.3% higher than last year. Indicators: In the indicator that records the degree of convenience for the operation provided by the economy, for the operation of a business, for the start of a business activity - indicators taken into account in order to select all potential investors around the world, the World Bank’s “Doing Business” indicators – Greece in 2019 recorded the seventy-second position and was on the line of ranking in the richest OECD countries. In particular, the situation is even worse. In the ease of business, ladies and gentlemen, our country has the twenty-seventh place in Europe of the twenty-eight. At the time and at the cost of installing electricity, our country has the fourth place in Europe of twenty-eight. In time and in cost for the property statement, our country is twenty-eight in eight. In trade outside the borders 20th to twenty-eight and in the speed and effectiveness of justice is in the twenty-seventh place at twenty-eight. I am tabling the “Doing Business 2019” report for the record, with the indicators mentioned above. [[At this point, the Member of Parliament, Mr Ioannis Economou, presents for the minutes the above document, which is in the archives of the Secretariat Department of the Directorate for Stenography and Practice of the Parliament.]] Last but not least, part of this puzzle, ladies and gentlemen, is the two and a half thousand investment projects waiting for its turn in the shelves and offices of ministries, it is the months and years that candidate investors expect to obtain environmental licenses, and it is also the paranoia of being able to take different responses from the same services of different structures on the same subject. Yes, different responses from the same services of different structures! In other words, to answer a region's service otherwise the same department of the decentralised region in relation to the same subject. Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, with the draft 'Invest in Greece' bill, we are trying to change this image. First of all, we reform the framework and speed up the procedures for investment projects and development aid. We are broadening both the category of investments that can be characterised as strategies - and the PPPs in this category - and the objects that can be strengthened. The evaluation procedures for investment projects are speeding up, since the possibility of private and private services being exploited and the provision for specific deadlines to be met, we put an end to end to ending suffering procedures, and we put an end to the phenomenon of roaming projects and investors from Anna to Kaiafa without result. The dates for the procedures provided for will be respected, checked and will be the responsibility of the Minister of Development within one month, once all other deadlines have elapsed, justifying the adoption or rejection of an intervention. I note that there is nothing missing in the role of the State, its regulatory and auditing bodies, since sampling checks and effective control mechanisms are introduced. Secondly, with an organised, innovative and coherent framework of actions we face this great scourge. The National Observatory for the Planning and Simplification of Procedures is established, which includes all actions to simplify administrative procedures on the part of the Greek state. The Written Observatory of the Ministry of Digital Policy, which seeks and captures the administrative burdens from the functioning of the legislation, which harm citizens, public administration and businesses, is being established. With a number of provisions in the GFCM, we simplify the lives of entrepreneurs. In five days' time, GEMH services must decide. We allow certified notaries to intercede between businessmen and GEMI. Most of the operations that will be published will be of a declaratory nature by entrepreneurs. We remove the two services for publication and evaluation of the services of public limited companies and leave only the GGE service. I would like to say here, Minister, that the Government should positively see the proposal made by the chambers so that the operation of the GFCM services is not compulsory for three people and that they are headed by PDO or TE, but left to the discretion of the Chambers to determine it. Thirdly, the Single Digital Charter and the Infrastructure Register are established, ladies and gentlemen. The Single Digital Charter will include information on all geographical data, which will clarify which economic activity is permitted in which area. In this way, at a second stage, we will be driving faster and more efficiently into the licensing process, as the data will be known, available, and state, public authorities, namely candidate investors, will speak the same language. The Infrastructure Register, on the other hand, puts an end to a regime in which we had facilities and infrastructure, after years, to remain orphaned and to keep them all responsible and ultimately no one. Fourthly, authorisations are simplified. In particular, as regards environmental licensing, the parallel system of two classifications is abolished, with nuisances and classes. We keep, we choose the issue of environmental classification with categories A1, A2 and B. It is what incorporates all recent technological and scientific developments and contains purely environmental criteria. Simplify the procedure to determine whether a modernised undertaking requires new environmental conditions or not. Fifth, we motivate business to set up a business park. Environmental licensing is facilitated in business parks, which cover in an organised manner activities that may cause a burden to the environment. For an undertaking established in a business park which already has authorisations, it is sufficient for a simple notification that it commences its activity. The establishment of new medium-term nuisance industries in organised activities in Attica, which was not allowed in the past, despite the fact that it is provided for in the Attica Regulatory Plan. Six, we regulate labour market issues. Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, with our work interventions, this multi-brand development plan, we are moving towards the adoption of sound rules on the labour market. Away from idealism and from ostrichism we confirm and broaden the collective autonomy of the social partners, while at the same time enabling jobs to be safeguarded in enterprises in critical economic conditions. In particular, the new law, harmonising between the others and the existing European practice - particularly countries which have very advanced systems of collective labour relations, collective agreements and a developed social state - gives the additional opportunity to social partners to decide on their own the scope of the sectoral conventions they sign and to exclude, when they agree, from the application of the companies which have proved to be facing major economic problems. Such a law defines, describes, to the extent that undertakings in bankruptcy proceedings, in bankruptcy or bankruptcy proceedings and out-of-court settlements. Accordingly, they will also be applied in order to bring together collective arrangements and to extend them. Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, I should like to take this opportunity to say a little more. To interpret the government's choice by means of this regulation, one must clearly answer a single question. We have an operation that collapses. What are we gonna do? Are we going to give the institutional opportunity, by maintaining wages above national collectives and employment clauses, to save the company, or are we going to let it collapse? Close the business, lose the state's taxes and insurance contributions, stay on the road. That's the question. And our institutional intervention is self-evident in choosing to help save jobs and jobs. Be careful not to get us wrong. Here we do not have an ideological debate on how to allocate profits or privileges to businesses and jobs that are going well, where everything goes beforehand. We are discussing whether the legislator, if the state, will allow institutional entrepreneurs and workers to agree on a different tactic, ensuring the minimums for workers in order to rescue the operation. Ladies and gentlemen, we do not want and, of course, we shall not allow this possibility given to the social partners, to become a vehicle for generalized wage reductions. We are here - and the Government, Minister, and the New Democracy Parliamentary Group - to ensure in its implementation the philosophy and meaning of this regulation. And to protect both workers and society from any volunteers who will try to canvass the new institutional potential that the institutional framework gives. Keep working. With two innovative arrangements - the electronic register of trade unions and employers and the optional electronic voting option - we increase transparency and participation in the trade union movement. In particular, I would like to say that it will include details - and I have discussed them in detail - its statutes and amendments, the results of the elections, the number of members involved in the register, the number of workers from the organisations involved in these undertakings, which are not personal data and which are therefore not linked to the protection of trade union freedom. One understands, ladies and gentlemen, that these two interventions, in addition to all others, also strengthen the prestige of the trade union movement. Because, ladies and gentlemen, anyone who believes that scenes like the one we lived in the summer, with GSEE to tour all over Greece, will be able to hold a conference and with PAME to pursue it in close proximity to planes, ravines, trains, and complaints from both sides about bulls and trade union mafias and monkeys, are honoring the trade union movement, I think it is moving out of reality. Our work interventions still include provisions to protect precarious work. So we increase by 12% the cost of extra work, overtime, in such forms of parttime work, while remaining fines for undeclared work. We are giving the way out of the fine to two thousand, if the businessman hires the undeclared worker, and at the same time we are establishing the mandatory re-control of undeclared labour enterprises and the register of such businesses. Seventh: A new framework for the licensing and control of antenna constructions on land is established through the multi-project. This is how we manage to shorten half a year, from two to one year - this is the assessment of all the relevant bodies - which will go ahead and implement these procedures. Furthermore, Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, with the regulations of the draft law the judicial procedure is speeding up with the administrative courts and the Council of State. The establishment of the Hellenic Investment and External Trade Company is planned. Better use is made of the resources of the Public Investment Programme which do not go for co-financed projects through the introduction of the national development programme, under which the sectoral and regional development programmes go respectively. The license is simplified for a number of enterprises of health interest in the central markets of Athens. Free access to higher education is abolished. Provisions concerning the standards of schools are laid down. We liberate postgraduate studies at universities from the tight embrace of the Ministry of Education and implement operational arrangements on issues of vocational equivalence. The online betting and electronic games market is still regulated, the system of research institutes is being modernised and the National Board of Research, Technology and Innovation is being established. Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, I would like to end with three main comments. Observation First: "Investing in Greece" creates a development framework, which includes the entire range of economy and entrepreneurship. Because, ladies and gentlemen, the fight against bureaucracy, the speeding up and simplification of licensing, the householding of business parks, the facilitation of investment in 5G networks and telecommunications, the acceleration of decisions and administration and justice, the clarification of what is permitted and where, the protection of precarious work, are regulations and changes that concern everyone. It is obviously about large and very large investments, but it is also about the many small and medium-sized investments that dare hundreds of entrepreneurs and family and small and medium-sized enterprises in our country. They are about investors who make life easier. However, they also concern the protection of public money and of workers with effective control mechanisms and operating mechanisms which it provides for. The second point concerns the criticism of our bill. Our bill has also been well-considered, especially with regard to Members' concerns about whether environmental respect is in line with the changes we have made to speed up procedures. These views and questions have been answered. But above all, to be honest, in order to cover the impossibility of formulating a substantive opposition to a law which, in fact, recommends the imposition of common sense on absurdity, we have seen on the part, above all, of the Officerial Response, repeating one kilowattage of trout. What did SYRIZA blame us for? He accused us of photographic arrangements and benefits. He blamed us for an environmentally indifferent policy, for a policy that fully confers the role of the State in private enterprises, to individuals whom SYRIZA considers in advance a suspect of poaching. He accused us of a policy that prepares general wage reductions. We have heard, ladies and gentlemen, that we are putting specific business interests into development law. And we discovered from the government's explanations that the codes of these enterprises are excluded. Not included. We have heard, ladies and gentlemen, that the development law will take on sponsorship as a sponsor of television broadcasts. And we have seen from the government's explanations and from the public debate that our predictions are about upgrading technology to telecommunications companies. We have heard that Union law does not take account of it. And we have seen that our environmental forecasts go beyond the European Union's predictions and forecasts. We have also heard that we are closing our eyes to colleges as far as professional equivalence is concerned. And we have seen that we are reintroducing a regime that was in place until May 2019, that is, four and a half years over the days of the previous government. Ladies and gentlemen, this criticism is a critical adolescence, it is an ideal criticism and it is also a deeply hypocritical criticism. This is a sculpture of SYRIZA both with the past and with its mirror. And it's on the sidelines of our own concept and our own development line. I should like to close, Mr President, on the third and final point: Ladies and gentlemen, after last July's elections there is a new opportunity in the country, a great opportunity. A chance for growth, for more jobs, for better incomes. But in today's world, ladies and gentlemen, growth is not being ordered. And investments cannot come as a product of self - astonishing prophecy. She needs a plan. It needs a framework for definitive and holistic treatment of the causes and outbreaks that create counterincentives and block investments. Our government instead of inaction has chosen the action. Instead of bourgeoisism and majestics we have chosen to locate, we have chosen to review law - law, article - with the dozens of distortions that make businesses and investors difficult in this place. We have chosen to create an attractive development framework that will attract large and small investors to test their ideas and forces. We have chosen to create a development framework that will allow many more Greeks to find jobs and increase their incomes. And that is why I call on you to vote in favour of this bill. Thank you very much.

In the second committee, now, we were initially placed in the loss of two positions of Greece in terms of its international competitiveness, because the Minister responsible criticised the previous government on this. We said this in summary: the aim of the Greek Government today is to increase our country to the index of global competitiveness, including through the new development bill. This bill gives literally land and water to investors and leftover banks at the expense of the citizens. With complete liberalisation of the labour market, by deregulation of collective agreements and by abolishing all kinds of rules on multinationals. Now, according to the organization that scores the competitiveness of each country, a good position means long-term potential for growth. But what is true about the members of the organisation who compile the indicator? Simplely an institution in which the thousands of strongest multinationals in the world are involved, with an important budget, funded by the hundred strategic partners of the organisation, while strategic partners can only become leaders of international sectors of the world economy with long-term good governance and aim to improve the situation of the world. That's how it's described on the institution's website, and it's not funny, as you might assume. Among other strategic partners are Deutsche Bank, the criminal gang in the German police itself, and the US ‘AIG’, which was among the companies that triggered the 2008 financial crisis and were rescued by taxpayers. It is difficult, then, to believe that they have good corporate governance or that their goal is to improve the conditions on the planet. While the entire structure of the institution reminds of the corresponding international transparency based in Germany and its members of the most corrupt companies in the world. By analyzing, now, the methodology of the competitiveness index, one finds that it operates using factors that do not directly affect competitiveness but indirectly through the returns on investment that the institution considers to be the key growth levers. It is therefore considered that the aim of the best possible profit of companies is synonymous with the objective of rising growth, which means that everything that increases the profits of multinationals investing in each country. They are positive for its competitiveness and lead to its growth. In summary, the country's high score means for multinationals: Firstly, their investments will be highly profitable. Secondly, that much of these returns will not be taken to workers through wages or even to the public through taxes but to their own funds, and thirdly, that they will not be faced with annoying laws and regulations such as environmental regulations. There is now an impressive similarity between the countries in the upper positions of the competitiveness index - that is, where Greece wants to go - with those which are also placed in the first positions of the shadow index drawn up by the organisation of a network of tax justice, the Tax Justice Network, i.e. the higher the competitiveness index each country is, the more unfair each country is in terms of its ordinary citizens, which is probably not a coincidence. For example, countries such as Switzerland, Singapore, Luxembourg, the Netherlands, the United States and Germany are firmly in the first place in ranking the competitiveness index. Obviously because they have the necessary infrastructure, which helps multinationals to undermine tax legislation and transparency rules. The indicator, therefore, essentially measures the importance of the various states worldwide for multinationals, so that they can choose which country best serves their interests. One of the individual factors of the competitiveness index is flexibility in the definition of wages. In this case, a country has a low degree when it has a central procedure to determine the wages of workers by means of collective agreements, while a high degree when each undertaking alone can determine the wages. In addition, free redundancies without compensation lead to higher positions as regards their classification in the global competitiveness index. [[The President-in-Office of the Council, Mr HARALABOS Athanasios, takes over the President-in-Office of the Council, Mr HARRAMPOS Athanasou.]] The Greek Government, therefore, with a view to increasing the country's ranking in the competitiveness index, in order to achieve the growth of foreign investment, plans for the complete abolition of the laws and rules on multinationals, among other things, with the new development bill. In other words, the provision of tax avoidance, the abolition of collective agreements, the facilitation of redundancies, the abolition of environmental or archaeological obstacles, etc. If it succeeds above, with the advantage of the fact that its previous government has opened the way by convincing the Greeks, unfortunately, that there is no other solution, then Greece will indeed have a much better place in the competitiveness index, attracting investment that, however, will not benefit the citizens at all, but will impoverish them even further. In simple terms, it will increase the GDP of the country but at the same time reduce the standard of living of the population, which, unfortunately, the citizens will see when it is too late, as has happened, in many other countries. There is, however, one problem to which the government has not given the necessary importance or simply hides it from us. The fact that the attraction of foreign multinationals requires particular tax treatment, such as by Ireland, to which they are not taxed at the rate of 12.5%, such as all other companies, but at much lower than after they have been negotiated with the authorities. ParlaMint-GR_2019-10-23-S1-commons.u99	Thank you, Mr President. [[Applause from the New Democracy wing]] In this bill, we have a number of working provisions, which hit at their core once again collective bargaining and collective agreements. They hit the two basic principles, the principle of more favourable regulation and the principle of expansion, in the same way as the second memorandum, i.e. with surgical blows to sectoral collective labour agreements and arbitration. It was precisely these two points on which the second memorandum insisted. It is obvious that this is where a mechanism for the compression of wages and labour rights is created. It is a stated objective, a stated objective of the second memorandum. He's actually a stated target of this bill too. It is not just the experience of 2012 to remind us of all this, where we had an eruption of business collective agreements that most of them predicted wage cuts. There is no need for the experience of that social disaster with the simultaneous explosion of unemployment. The very scientific text which the government invokes as the only scientific basis for what is being proposed today, namely the text of the Bank of Greece, clearly says on page 4 that we have a mechanism that will lead to wage reductions. Another analysis from there and beyond neoliberalism, that this will have growth potential. However, it's a stated target. We agree on what Mr Loverdos said, namely that there are a number of unconstitutionalities and in many other parts of the bill we shall always support them. For our part, however, we want to insist on one - because, in our view, it is clear - that of arbitration and it is clear because we also have the relatively recent unanimous decision of the Council of State. A word on the constitutional issue. At 22.2 it is clear that the constitutional legislature has made a choice for the priority of collective labour relations arrangements, that is to say, labour relations, as a priority, to be regulated by collective agreements and that is why there is the principle of subsidiarity of state regulation. It is precisely because it wants this priority that they should be regulated by collective agreements, by decisions between employers and workers with the result of collective autonomy, that it has put all the effort into the matter of arbitration. When we say that it is dressed with the arbitration box, it means that the activation of arbitration does not presuppose the agreement and consensus of both parties. If it were to be said, there would be no special reference to referral of the Constitution to arbitration. If, in other words, the parties had to agree to go to arbitration, that would be within collective autonomy anyway. That is to say, as they agree on a number of other issues, they agree to go to arbitration as well. But that is not what the constitutional legislator wants. It also wants the possibility of unilateral referral to arbitration. All this was said very clearly by the Plenary Assembly of the Council of State in its unanimous decision in 2014, where there unanimously decided that the regulation of the second memorandum abolishing the right to unilateral referral to arbitration was unconstitutional. And the court said in particular that if the referral to arbitration were to be understood only as consensual, the specific reference to the constitutional text of arbitration would be unnecessary and also that the constitutional purpose of resolving disputes is not served if part of the collective dispute, refusing to accept arbitration, is in fact capable of canceling its solution at a collective level. So it says the Council of State that both the letter and the spirit of the Constitution protect the right of unilateral recourse to arbitration. What's the government doing? How's the legislator doing with Article 57? It effectively abolishes the right to unilateral referral to arbitration. It sets so many conditions where in practice the vast majority of workers will be impossible to have the right to unilateral access. It says that this can only be done if it concerns public utilities, so it excludes a huge number of workers from having access to arbitration. It says that this can only be done if there is talk of greater public economic interest - who will judge him and when will he be? - that this case is not already covered by the first concerning only the ECJs. It adds another precondition that only if any other means of trade union activity has been exhausted can they appeal to arbitration. So, trade union action, what? The strike? Does the legislator make the strike condition for referral to arbitration? But the very meaning of arbitration is to make work peace through collective agreements so that we do not have to get to trade union action. Does this make trade union action a prerequisite to go to arbitration? It is clear that we have a number of conditions here which make the right to unilateral recourse to arbitration completely inactive. So we have a regulation which affects its core the right of unilateral referral to arbitration. And the worst of all is that we have the legislator coming here to abolish the decision of the Council of State by law. What rule of law, and what distinction do we have here? It is a flagrant anti-constitutional provision and we insist on it, precisely because it supports it and the Council of State, and it will certainly have to be withdrawn.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-10-23-S1-commons.u100	Thank you, Mrs. Ahchioglu. I would like to ask the Vice-President of the New Republic, Mr Buga, to take the floor.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-10-23-S1-commons.u101	Thank you, Mr President. Mr President, I should first of all like to say that the bill removes a number of obstacles which have created a problem for development. It's not anti-developmental, as Mr. Loverd said. Now, as far as the issues of unconstitutionality are concerned, I will begin with the provisions that the Change Movement says as supposedly unconstitutional. It is the provision of Article 53(8), 55 and 56(4). These provisions, in accordance with the explanatory memorandum to the bill, ladies and gentlemen, facilitate collective agreements in the current operating conditions of undertakings, the survival of which is difficult. In other words, this regulation widens the scope of the choices made by the social partners so that they can formulate in free negotiations specific working and remuneration conditions in specific enterprises which prove to be particularly difficult, so that they cannot respond to the functioning of sectoral labour contracts. In particular for undertakings referred to in Article 53, which are in bankruptcy, bankruptcy or non-judicial conciliation arrangements. Here, Mr Loverdos also refers to the issue of unconstitutionality, namely that clear and concrete legislative powers are not contained. In this respect, Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, the following is to be said: companies must be in a difficult economic situation and are in particular under special circumstances, i.e. a regime of bankruptcy, bankruptcy or an out-of-court settlement. In other words, there is an automatic reference to the clear and specific terms of those provisions, under which an undertaking may be placed under bankruptcy or bankruptcy procedure or out-of-court settlement. So the delegation is quite clear, because it refers to the criteria that are strictly and legally defined, so that the joint legislature can judge and, of course, judge by the parties, by the social partners, but also by the courts, if necessary and present, which companies are involved. Consequently, the criteria are fully defined by the provisions of the laws which - I repeat - define the conditions for going into this regime and do not raise any question of the unconstitutionality of these provisions, which, Mr President, I must say, are fully in line with both the letter and the spirit of Article 22 of the Constitution, and in particular with Article 5 of the Constitution for the reasons I have mentioned and do not want to repeat, and concern the collective possibility of negotiating and strengthening, in particular, workers in particular, to negotiate conditions and working conditions. Now, as far as Article 57 is concerned, I think that they either read and did not understand or understand and misinterpreted the decision of the Council of State. This decision, in particular the provisions of Article 57, is not in breach of Article 22. Article 22, even by simple reading, shows that it wants the joint legislator to lay down rules restricting the referral to arbitration, which is a last resort to resolving collective disputes. Both the theory, in which I have had a little time, Mr President, to look back, as well as the taut case-law of the Council of State, accepts that the legislator is the primary body of competence for matters relating to industrial relations. The legislator must intervene as long as his intervention is necessary for the treatment and service of a more general interest, which is constitutionally guaranteed and is different from that - that is, differently - served by collective autonomy. This means that the institution of arbitration must function as an alternative and as a last resort means of resolving the dispute. With this in mind, then, and taking into account the recent recommendations made by the International Labour Organisation, there is the possibility of unilateral recourse to arbitration as a last resort in strictly defined cases, which are laid down in the law and with criteria that are absolutely specific, defined by law, and I repeat that very strictly. All this, why are they set? In order to preserve an overriding public interest. We must also take into account here - and I say this for those who are not legal - and the limits of judicial control. What has the Council of State accepted? What are the limits of judicial review? It is the overrun and the error in the assessment of the public interest. So the Supreme Court's judgment is whether there is any error in overcoming the public interest. Here too, Mr President, the constitutional criteria are quite clear. And the legal criteria are laid down and I refer to Article 25 of the Constitution, the principle of proportionality. This will also be overthrown by the Council of State, and we must also bear in mind, as a guiding principle of whether the Council of State is serving a greater public interest. Therefore, taking into account what I have said, the provision of Article 57 does not only contravene the case-law of the Council of State, but is fully in line with it and there is no problem of constitutionality. Both objections must be rejected as unfounded. Thank you, Mr President.

This text is a multi-project that regulates and solves problems, overcomes obstacles, lays the foundations for the development of our national economy, tackling public function as a single set. Secondly, we ensure that we do not again meet in the same position. In other words, we'll cure the problems, we won't just patch them. Thirdly, that we know very well that in order to achieve this, we must strengthen the investment initiative, public and private. Today the many and significant investment barriers that exist mainly in licensing issues, but also environmental, urban and labour issues, are removed. Issues that come as a bad dowry from the past, which set up walls and operate deterrently to attract investment, are resolved. A large number of small reforms are concentrated, but equally important, which change the business environment, work encouragingly and help new businesses or old ones who face financial problems. We solve problems, we don't hide them under the carpet, and we don't create any new ones. This regulation solves major problems, especially in my area, in the Pella Prefecture, which relieves a large part of citizens mainly in Almopia, where the occupation of logging is fairly developed. In other words, the intention of the New Democracy Government, unlike the previous one, is to solve problems, correct bad texts, facilitate and simplify procedures. Ladies and gentlemen, the development bill which is now being called on to vote in favour of this House lays the foundations for Greece of the future. ParlaMint-GR_2019-10-23-S1-commons.u207	Thank you, Mrs. Chabika Iatridis. The Minister of State and Digital Governance has the floor, Mr Pierracakis.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-10-23-S1-commons.u208	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, the Plenary Assembly of the Greek Parliament is called upon to vote on a draft law whose title testifies, not only the content but also the targeting: 'I invest in Greece'. This is a draft law that has been touching on all aspects of the investment process and is then introduced, not only by a formal consultation, but also by an informal dialogue, a dialogue which has been taking place for several years since the crisis, and which concerns what kind of country we want, what kind of productive model we want and whether the country will succeed in getting out of the crisis in the end with a different model from that through and because of which it has been in the crisis, that is to say, through the non-marketable sectors of the economy in the outward sectors of the economy, in exports, in direct foreign investment, in innovation, in all which our country can do, but which is still not. And if we had to make a ratio from the IT field, we would say that our country is a country with the right material, but with problematic software, with the right hardware, but with the wrong software, with all those advantages in its location, with the natural advantages, with human resources, but with institutions not always well targeted, not always properly oriented. And the hardest job to change our productive model is no more than changing software than changing the rules of the game. This draft law includes a number of provisions on the competence of the Ministry of Digital Governance. We have talked in detail about this in committee. I will mention, for example, the single digital map, which will collect geographical data, which are currently scattered, often non-digitalised, not only in the central state but also in the OTAs and these data should be able to be collected, digitised, to provide citizens free of charge in a simple, valid and complete way, for example, existing land uses, construction conditions, orgymotomy. Secondly, we are establishing what we have already said, the single digital portal of public administration, the GOFE.gr, which will gather the scattered digital public services, which will gradually become more and more through interoperability and we have already said that in the first few months of next year a first form of this portal will be available to citizens. Thirdly, the national framework for simplifying procedures, which will be the guide to recording and simplifying each administrative procedure, with the aim of horizontally combating bureaucracy, which is the basic work of this new Ministry and, finally, the new Directorate-General for cybersecurity, the new structure that will essentially be the national coordinator for national cybersecurity policy, now within the general telecommunications secretariat. Where I would like to stand a little above is in most of the provisions concerning the Ministry in the draft law, regarding the licensing of antenna systems. And this initiative is a good example of intervention that comes to correct a chronic distortion, which has existed since the 1990s, when mobile telephony began in our country. And I'll start from something that's not included in the draft law, but which's what's involved most of them, which's about the radiation of antenna systems. In Greece we have six and a half thousand antennas. Sixteen and a half thousand antennas are located in fourteen and a half thousand unique locations. There is an international organisation, ICNIRP, which defines international radiation limits. Greece has legislated to move to lower radiation than international limits. We move to 70% of these radiations, and where there is a school or hospital, within 300 meters, we move to 60% of the international limit. Every year there are checks. Last year the SEA, the Greek Atomic Energy Commission carried out two and a half thousand tests. How many antennas were above the limit? Just four. And certainly not above the international limits. It was above our Greek limits which are lower. It is a matter of agony, and it is a fact that we need to have huge care for health issues or issues relating to the protection of citizens. This issue, however, is an issue that we have arranged with additional care on the part of the Greek state. What's changing in the antenna? The licensing process is changing, because there was an absurdity there. For example, very often an antenna changed from 3G to 4G and had to go through all the licensing. There was, in other words, a gray regime, which also had evidence of irrationality. For example, it must re-enter building permits for an already spaceed antenna. Such absurdities by the existing draft law, by these provisions, are resolved. And it is a necessary treaty to be solved, in and out of another prism. It is not just a prism here that we have resolved an outstanding situation that has existed for years. The prism is forward, and it is about how we get through this new era. We have the fifth generation networks in front of us. We're listening everywhere about the 5G. This is an issue that is debated internationally, and is also discussed under other conditions. Greece must not miss this train too. So, that's where we have a dilemma. We will either respond to the new operational data or stay in a grey situation and miss this train too. So our message is clear. With existing changes, antennas are either licensed or demolished. Gray ends. Ladies and gentlemen, this draft law comes to a vote shortly after the first hundred days of the new government are completed, it comes to a framework regarding our Ministry and to complete its institutional tools, those which were first shaped by Law 4623/2019 and which are a completion of the first phase of a legislative cycle for us. We could talk about an end to the principle. This concept of the first hundred days has a great historical symbolism, starting with Roosevelt, starting in the 1930 ’ s and the thirteenth Roosevelt bills of 1933 that introduced the new deal and pushed America out of its very great crisis. In our first hundred days we've done too much and that's a qualitative difference. They are identified more with the end of the crisis than with the middle or beginning. That is precisely why, now, it is not a question of overcoming the crisis, but of planning how we go from now on. More than a hundred days ago, the question is where the country will go in the next ten years. And with the latest technological advances, it is certain that we will soon see things ahead of us, which look like a science fiction. For example, speaking of the fifth generation networks, many of their applications already, if anyone sees them how they develop, seem to come from far away in the future. To anyone who is sceptical, what I would urge him to do, it would be to compare 1993 to 2003 and how our life has changed from the Internet in a decade. Changes will now be even more rapid and cannot be supported by institutions, structures and networks which have been designed for the past period than we have been living for. The fourth industrial revolution will bring about even greater changes. So the first step is to remove the obstacles of the past. Such actions result from the proposed legislative changes, and I believe that in this light we should also address the overall draft law to be adopted, by answering the question of how we want Greece to be in ten years' time from now. Through a set of initiatives leading to the investment path, which is also the cornerstone of much-needed growth for many years in the winter. Before I place myself on the bill, I would like to make five important points, which call for the need to speed up initiatives that pave the way for investment in order to raise our economy to deal effectively and permanently with the consequences of the economic crisis in our country. Element number one: Unemployment. Our country has the highest unemployment rate in the European Union, as it reaches 17.2%, while the average of the European Union does not exceed 6.3. This means that there must be incentives to develop and encourage entrepreneurship in order to develop businesses which, however, must strictly follow the legal rules relating to labour law, competition and the restriction of unfair competition. Item 2: Part-time. Parttime work currently reaches 9% of all employees, while in 2007 it did not exceed 5.3%. There are reasonable indications that a large proportion of parttime workers are employed in full time, without the employer being paid the corresponding contributions. Third element: Exports. There is a discrepancy between exports and imports, resulting in a large trade deficit in our country. It is worth noting that the trade balance deficit is increasing by 6.3%, corresponding to EUR 1.8 billion between the time limit: July 2018-July 2019. Item four: Investments. Investments in our country have literally collapsed, given that in 2007 they reached 26% of GDP and amounted to EUR 60 billion, in 2018 they collapsed to 11.1% of GDP on the basis of net investments, which are negative after 2011. In general, investment as a percentage of GDP is the lowest among the countries of the European Union and percentaged as follows: 1.9% of investment in our country compared with 3.5% in the European Union, while the investment gap reaches 100 billion euros. Element fifth: The ranking position of our country based on the Doing Business index of the global bank. Greece takes the 72th position of this indicator and the last in the rich economies of OECD members. The Doing Business indicator highlights the degree of convenience that an economy provides in order to start operating a business and take serious account of the potential investors. Consequently, this legislative initiative comes to eliminate the monster of bureaucratic processes that acted as a brake on any attempts by candidate investors, raising huge and successive obstacles to any investment initiative. To deal with the slow administration of justice, to clarify the incomprehensible provisions of a large number of legislation, and to fill in the gaps in existing legislation, which has proved to be very difficult for the development of entrepreneurship to a large extent. And it even comes to reduce its operating and environmental licensing procedures, their time-consuming and cost-effective effects. Therefore, this draft law gives flexibility to the area of strategic investment, it makes significant changes to development laws in order to complete control and certification of a large number of investment projects. Investment laws to date, instead of being used as a useful development tool, have enclosed investors in a series of improper procedures resulting in the loss of resources and jobs. The Single Digital Charter is created which enables all interested parties to know on what specific points economic activity is permitted. The National Infrastructure Register, which will function in a digital format and will provide information to any interested party. Inside it, buildings and all public infrastructures as well as important information such as when they were built will be registered, when control and maintenance operations have been carried out and which body is responsible for these responsibilities. Entrepreneurial parks are created and significant incentives are given to enterprises operating in these organised infrastructures. Discard the nuisance steps in the manufacturing industries and are replaced by the environmental classification categories so as to avoid double licensing. The National System of Quality of Infrastructure by public private entities is established in order to address fragmented legislation on business obligations. The Writing Observatory is established, which is an important tool for its effective response while abolishing Law 3419/2005 and being replaced by new provisions. The World Bank’s Doing Business Index ranks Greece in the seventeenth second place among a hundred nine economies. We are lasting in Europe for 2019. With personal experience, coming from a family that is conscientious of entrepreneurship and production, I know the value of the changes that we are discussing today. In the ears of the citizens, all these changes may not mean much, but for anyone who has attempted this bill in Greece it is key. In short, the authorisation procedure for starting an industrial activity is being simplified. Incentives are given to the establishment of enterprises in a single business park. An alternative procedure is introduced by auditors or audit firms. The process of certification of the completion and start of the production operation of investments is considerably accelerated. A major reform is introduced by the provisions governing procedures in the General Commercial Register, known to all GFCM. This is a point of the bill that touches me personally, since I have been a legal adviser to the Larissa Chamber for many years and I am first aware of the daily problems of the trader and the free trader. At the same time, each freelance and businessman's demand has always been to simplify procedures in his dealings with the state. This is precisely what we are doing through the extensive limitation of the legal acts and items, through the automated registration that will now be carried out by the responsible, through the expansion of GEMI services by conferring competence on the notaries. The proposed provisions drastically reduce bureaucracy by consolidating the legality and registration process within a strict timeframe by GFCM. ParlaMint-GR_2019-10-23-S1-commons.u401	Thank you very much, Mr President. Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, we have listened so long. We have heard strong criticism of the existing bill by SYRIZA, KKE, Mer25 and Greek Solution colleagues and we have heard a lot about development. The development, therefore, comes in response to the issues of cruel reality and not with arguments of a moral nature, which we have heard just now and not of course by shouting out slogans. So it's inappropriate or useless at least. Development wants systematic study and substantial dialogue with those who are in the production process, with those who face the real obstacles, with the real obstacles and often with the law toward them, but not their own bourgeoisie. We've heard various slogans and implied ones. "We are with the people and not with capital," some said, which is something that has no place today. The capital is created by the people and by the one who produces it. After hearing various wastes, I, at least, respond with the saying of Willie Brad that 'their profits are today's investment and day-to-day jobs', which is so timely than ever for the existing bill. Now let's go talk about the existing bill. The law to be passed shows without doubt that this government was ready to take over the country's rulership. The failures of the institutional framework had been identified. Proposals had been drawn up, including the producer bodies, business and experts. In a very short time, therefore, since the takeover of governance, a comprehensive and detailed draft was presented before us, first, with surgical accuracy, to make critical adjustments to the existing institutional framework, in order to liberate the country's development dynamics, while introducing new institutions that will give solutions to chronic development problems, such as the Single Digital Map of Geographical Data. We are now going to three basic things, three key words, which are also the mark of this bill. First of all, I should like to simplify, secondly, rationalise and thirdly reorganise. Firstly, the draft law promotes development by simplifying procedures, removing obstacles to the development of business and investment activity. Secondly, rationalising the functioning of institutions so that they can function efficiently. And thirdly, as a whole, redeeming the exercise of various activities, removing stumbling blocks to give air for creation and investment in Greece that it produces. Now, the existing bill essentially supports major social problems primarily in the workplace. This is an answer to the JRC's colleagues, because you cannot have an improvement in the business environment unless you protect your employees in business so that it is a business environment not just development, but a healthy environment. On the other hand, genuine protection of private sector workers is not achieved by unnecessary bureaucracy and overregulation, which is choking companies, and does not restrict the abuse of rights. So, beyond development, the existing draft law also promotes institutional innovation. New institutions are being set up which are reformist areas and provide solutions that have been expected for both the state and the citizen and for the professional. Now let's go see some important points of the bill for island Greece, but also for island local economies, which was also raised by the Chief of Mera 25. Looking at the law being passed under the filter of islands, I find some important interventions that pave the way for the islands' adapted development. Firstly, in the island area we have many small businesses in need of rapid, easy and effective procedures. For island businesses, therefore, the processing of procedures causes valuable man-time costs and expense due to scattered services on many islands. The current bill, therefore, introduces, firstly, accelerate procedures through GEMI, secondly, exploiting partnerships with notaries and thirdly, automated entries. Secondly, with the establishment of the National Development Programme for the development of national public investment resources, regions will now form a Strategic Public Investment Programme based on the needs of local municipalities. The Strategic Programme will complement funding through the ESRB and can thus be covered in a more flexible way with development needs, but also with infrastructure needs. Thirdly, the existing bill simplifys the conditions for municipalities to be able by public contracts to entrust private functions, such as waste collection, green spaces as well as electric lighting. So, actually, what's going on? The hands of the mayors are resolved so that by exploiting private individuals, along with any recruitment to human resources, they can provide efficient and quality services to the public. After all, the economic theory has now shown decades that the provision of services by public bodies is qualitatively worse and economically inconsistent with the services provided by the private economy. Fourthly, particularly in the island area with special relief, the particular spatial areas, as well as the protection areas, the problem of finding the accuracy, but also of checking the appropriate geographical data, finds a solution through the Single Digital Charter. [[At this point the Third Vice-President of Parliament takes over Mr Athanasios BOURAS]] Fifthly, we are going to introduce regulations and procedures into individual laws without an island clause, without taking into account the specificities of the islands. This involves, among other things, an excessive and often irrational bureaucratic burden on islands, but also on islands. So through the National Simplification Programme, we look forward to specific adaptations of procedures, mainly by exploiting digital governance, especially for island regions. Ladies and gentlemen, in any case we have an ambitious reform agenda in front of us. This bill marks a new era for Greece, restoring growth, attracting investments and creating new jobs. Only in this way will we get out of the vicious circle that has kept our country back for at least a decade. For all these reasons, Mr President, we are voting in favour of the bill.

It has been mentioned by the President that when an undertaking starts operating within a business park - I must make it clear to you that this is something that is happening throughout Europe and in America - the operation which only starts with a notification within the business park is the same as the operation of the business park. In other words, the business park is licensed for category B, which is the average nuisance, so under the new category A2, the company starts operation because it is the same ranking and has to prepare an environmental impact study. The obligation to prepare an environmental impact assessment is not removed at all. Now, let's say how business works. Businesses are starting either by notification, as we have said, or by operating authorisation. 80% of enterprises today in Greece only start with notification. This does not mean that they do not have to produce the permits. Therefore, in this bill, what the Minister said is the right one, that we are not undermining the concept of environmental licensing at all. Not only do we degrade it, but we strengthen it. All we are simplifying is that at the start of operation you don't need to collect all these cards, which were obliged to collect earlier, and it may start immediately. But the environmental impact study must have it in order to be able to function.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-10-23-S1-commons.u405	Ladies and gentlemen, it is true that Greece is thirsty for investment after a decade of disastrous economic crisis. Both society and the market expect through bold initiatives to establish clear rules in the relations between state, business and workers, remove barriers to investment, modernise and digitise public administration, to become a development leap, which is the national objective. And that's what the Prime Minister and the government do with this development plan. We are legislating fundamental changes and deep areas to reintroduce the 'radar' of attracting domestic and foreign investors. The arrangements of the 'Investing in Greece and other provisions' are in the sphere of common sense, in the direction of sustainable development with respect for the environment. Now is the crucial moment, which must strengthen the growth potential of the Greek economy, and that is what we are doing. Today many strategic investment for Greece remains on paper as their licensing is being delayed. Instead of acting as a development tool, investment laws entangle investors into administrative processes and delays, depriving them of valuable resources from the country and the economy and new jobs from society and citizens. The development draft law provides for greater flexibility in investment that is considered strategic, so that they can be quickly licensed. The scanning interventions contained in the bill allow for the formation of fertile soil and a friendly climate to attract new investments, leaving behind the obstacles of the past. In particular, it provides for the simplification of environmental authorisations, the facilitation of business within business parks, a single digital map, imprinting and concentration of all geographical data - for example, conditions and limitations on building, forests and forest land, aegean, archaeological sites, NATURA areas in a single digital system - and the creation of a digital land bank, the creation of a national register of infrastructure in which all the public buildings and infrastructures of the country will be presented. The process of integrating an industrial activity is also simplified and a national simplification programme is in place. The transactions for which the GFCM exercises control over more than one company shall be recorded and published automatically by the companies. Procedures for justice shall be speeded up by digitisation of legal instruments. There are anachronistic provisions, such as the provision of Law 4423/2006 concerning logging and all those who are engaged in and freely selling their goods, which were in existence. The labour and insurance provisions introduced to the vote in the development bill deal with crucial issues in the field of labour market and social security. The aim is to create a socially just and efficient social state. Among the important provisions - because I have no time to mention them all - are: in collective labour relations, in line with existing European practice, in particular in countries with advanced labour relations systems and a developed social state, by strengthening and broadening the scope of collective bargaining. In individual employment relationships, the employer's delay in paying wages is considered to be a harmful change in working conditions and therefore a contract is terminated but a time limit is entered. An additional 12% increase in the cost of the additional hours shall be increased if the agreed conditions for parttime work are exceeded. The role of the information system "ERGANI" in recording labour and employment is being upgraded. Undeclared work is being fought. We are definitely facing the unacceptable situation of the hundreds of thousands of outstanding pensions, which was and is a wound to the dignity of pensioners and the whole of society. Our aim is for at least two years from now, 90% of new pensions to be issued immediately. In the first phase, of course, by means of a special provision, the right to adopt pension decisions is conferred on the directors of regional services and organic structures and, secondly, by promoting a legislative initiative for the large digital jump ‘ATLAS’, since June 2020, new pensions will be issued digitally and directly. Ladies and gentlemen, it is true that the years of the crisis have been, especially in the region, a lack of new investments and there have been locks in many companies that once flourished. The Serres Prefecture as a gateway to and from the European Union paid comparatively the "marble" of deinvestment as a result of the crisis, and it has the sad privilege of occupying the last per capita GDP position among all the counties of the country. For many years now, town camps have been devastated and have not been given the opportunity for the Chamber to exploit the space of a camp for the creation of a business park or for the creation of a regional farm auction outside the urban web and next to the vertical axis of Egnatia Road, which will create multiplier benefits in the rural economy and entrepreneurship. There are strangles, bureaucracy, property regime and involved in the same city Ministries and local government, as if they were rivals. To dismantle the procedures in order to develop economic activities, we have to overcome such claws and bureaucratic processes that have cost the place expensive. The Government of Kyriakos Mitsotakis with the development plan and the tax bill, which aims to reduce taxes and which will be voted on in the next period, proceeds with a realistic plan on the path of development for a better future. And that is what our fellow citizens and those who naturally want to invest in Greece are aware of. And I believe that, with the favourable climate that exists, many will be those investors, after all that we are legislating on. Thank you.

These words also apply today, this debate. The assumption that investment and growth need as safe a environment as possible is self-evident nowadays. Two security factors would stand out: first, stable legal and fiscal framework. Secondly, political stability, which is highly dependent on economic stability. The lack of these has been in recent years and the great problem for our country, the fact that almost everything has been unpredictable in an insecure environment and, moreover, no investment incentives to change for the better. Today the juncture is indeed ideal. Recently, the Government of Kyriakos Mitsotakis has become self-sufficient, with a strong mandate to raise the economy and to shape the framework within which investment and wealth creation will benefit, which will bring many and better jobs, especially for young people. This political stability is a major comparative advantage for Greece, while most Eurozone countries are trying to preserve sensitive major government coalitions. We are one of the few countries in Europe that has a self-government government, ladies and gentlemen. The important bill, therefore, with two hundred thirteen provisions, with responsibility for seventeen ministries, is to respond to the need for a new framework of legislative and fiscal stability. It is the catalyst through which the country's productive forces will be activated and aims to remove the dozens of investment barriers to licensing issues. ParlaMint-GR_2019-10-23-S1-commons.u433	Thank you, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, we are discussing a bill, which has as its main aim to contribute to improving the country's competitiveness. In the research and innovation portfolio - which I have the responsibility of - there are important challenges ahead of us, and in this draft law we are taking a first small step towards tackling them. Today Greece is 20 in twenty-eight countries of the European Union in the European Innovation Index for 2019, and ranks forty-eight in twenty-nine countries in the corresponding global index. While we have the scientific potential, unfortunately we have not managed to use it to the extent that we should. As the Prime Minister has pointed out from the moment on, innovation will have a central role to play in the country's new development model, to face challenges such as climate change, competitiveness, the fourth industrial revolution, national security, digital transformation, health sciences and energy transition. Our bet is to implement an ambitious, but realistic, agenda and mobilise all available national forces in order to boost research and innovation by means of a vehicle to strengthen the country's international position. That is why Article 143 proposes the establishment of the National Council for Research, Technology and Innovation. The aim is to cover the gap between research and innovation. Unfortunately, today we have excellent research results, but low transformation of them into innovative products and services. Despite the fact that progress has been made, there is still a long way to go, if we want to have a leading role at international level and a leading role in the Balkans and Southeast Europe. The establishment of the ESRB is important because it will act as a state’s highest advisory body in the development of a national strategy for research, technology and innovation. Its task is to monitor national, European and international developments and to make proposals and to make public an annual national strategy for research and innovation, which we aspire to be the basis of planning for the government and all public administration. There are five major differences in the current pattern. Firstly, it will not consist solely of academics and researchers. Of course, Greek researchers and academics will participate. But internationally renowned personalities will participate in the market and the ecosystem of innovation, those who have experienced how an idea develops into a successful startup operation. There'll be two committees. The Committee on Research and Technology, which will focus on the issues of basic and applied research, and the Committee on Innovation and Intelligent Entrepreneurship, which will address issues of innovation development and improvement of the business environment in which newly developed enterprises, in other words, the development of a sustainable and hospitable ecosystem. Secondly, the ESRB will consist of eleven members, a number reduced significantly in relation to the current structure, because we want a flexible and functional structure. It is obvious that we do not want a decorative instrument, but a structure with scientists and technocrats from different academic fields, different institutions, both internal and external, and from different objects. Their common element will be to engage in national effort and their commitment to improving the country's international position in science and innovation. Thirdly, it was not in the past that the proposals on the country's strategic planning had to be made public. We now expressly foresee it, precisely because we want the people involved in cutting-edge technology to have a say on how we can make more substantial progress and society able to see and assess the proposals that are being made. Fourthly, for the first time, we ensure the participation of the Greek Research Union at the meetings of the ESRB. It is our belief that the country's highest advisory body on research should be involved in order to reconcile opinions with the state. Fifthly, the mission of the new institution includes encouraging public bodies to incorporate research results and innovative products and services created by supervised bodies of the General Secretariat of Research and Technology. In other words, we have the knowledge and the public administration must take advantage of it. Article 142 amends the provisions concerning the evaluation of the research centres and institutes in the country. The latest assessment has been made many years ago, which is a problem for the research centres themselves, which want to assess their scientific work. Unfortunately, while the process should have been launched and completed, there was no design at the Ministry in the previous years. We are therefore legislating to proceed with the process and to be evaluated by Greek and international legal persons with proven experience, know-how and international authority. I shall conclude, Mr President. With Article 145 we amend the organization of the Foundation of the Hellenic World in order to broaden the range of its activities. Ladies and gentlemen, as you can see from the provisions of the bill, the central objective is to improve what we can to make the state more effective. Our enemy is not any previous government. Our enthusiasm is the country's low position in all the indicators of competitiveness. With hard work we have already begun to improve the situation so that the country and the economy become competitive. I ask you to vote in favour of the provisions as a vote of confidence in a Greece that is changing, transforming and can look much more optimistic tomorrow. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-10-23-S1-commons.u451	Thank you, Mr President. Mr Ministers, ladies and gentlemen, unfortunately the long and deep economic crisis that we have experienced and the turbulent manipulations of the previous government have made our country the poorest state of Western democracies in Europe. Low per capita income, high unemployment rates, limited jobs and an ageing population compose the present image of Greece. We realise that it is imperative that the country returns to high and sustainable growth. An investment explosion is therefore needed, which will create new jobs and enhance the economic lives of the citizens. Overtaxation, uncertainty and restrictions on access to finance on competitive terms have, to date, been a blocking factor in attracting international investment. The current legislation, however, introduces a flexible process and creates a fertile business environment by means of provisions that abolish the reasons for distrust towards Greece, by facilitating the attraction and implementation of investments. It is known that the overall net position of foreign investors in the country remains lower among the Member States of the European Union. So the purpose of the bill we are discussing today is exactly what his title says: 'I invest in Greece'. The measures adopted are not empty, but their impact on GDP, investment and employment is estimated to be strong. According to a study by the Bank of Greece, since the provisions of the bill apply to their fullness, the minimum impact on GDP will be five points in the next ten years. What does that mean? Increase the standard of living of citizens, with fewer taxes, better social benefits and more jobs. Ladies and gentlemen, I have the honour of representing the Ioannina and I know very well what it means not to go ahead with major projects, not to create new jobs, not to be able to set up and operate a new business. Really, how many times have we seen the signatures drop, but the plays stay in the carpet or start and never end? Two months before the elections on 7 July, Mr Tsipras said from Ioannina that he had been tired of launching projects that had been delayed in previous years. I wonder if this statement was ironic to the citizens or self-sarcasm for his government's inertia. The bill speeds up by entrusting private chartered auditors to evaluate and monitor the implementation of investment projects. They are currently pouring two and a half thousand files that had been subject to development law. I have to say that we have already received the feeling of relief from businessmen from Ioannina and Epirus, who will finally see their investments from the archives and be completed thanks to the new regulations introduced by the current bill, because the New Democracy respects its election commitments, simplifying the procedures for strengthening entrepreneurship and the development of the country. We do not live in meaningless political controversy. We have worked out and put in place an integrated development plan instantly to proceed with the projects that have literally culminated in all these years and to develop the private initiative in all areas of social and economic life. But it's not the Ioannina, it's not just the Continent. It is the whole country that so much needs development. This situation must be addressed not by fragmentary arrangements, but by overall, systematic and effective means, and this is precisely what this multi-legislative plan aims to do. It aims at curing weaknesses that were left out of the poor and fragmented legacy and operate deterrently to attract investment, to sum up many small reforms that change the business environment as a whole, to remove dozens of investment barriers specifically licensed, environmental, town-building, to support newly-born businesses. This will create many well-paid jobs, so the country will return to high and sustainable growth. In addition, I would like to focus on the reforms that are under way in labour and digital governance. Starting with work, we are attempting a reform of the labour market in the current data for the benefit of workers and firms, in favour of full employment, the legality of the labour market and the increase in income in the insurance funds. Amongst other things, we are increasing by 12% the cost of extra hours if the agreed hours of work are exceeded, with a view to strengthening full-time work and the prevention of employers from systematic recourse to abused types of part-time contracts. We amplify the framework for combating undeclared work in the direction of strengthening full employment. The new legislation ensures the world of precarious work. In a strict and effective way, the legislative framework against undeclared work is reinforced, a social scourge which is afflicting the workers, but which is also deprived of income from the State and the insurance funds. We are creating an electronic register of infringibility and undeclared work for the first time. In addition, strong economic incentives are given to the offenders if they are directly employed by the undeclared worker. These reintroduction measures to legality strengthen the position of undeclared workers, strengthen employment with full employment and insurance rights and create conditions of stable employment for employees, without jeopardising already declared jobs. The Government of New Democracy shall develop and implement strategies and policies with specific objectives in order to effectively combat infringement in the labour market. The reforms of the bill at the level of digital governance are also extremely important. The current draft law promotes structural changes in the organisational units of the Ministry of Digital Governance, with the aim of reducing the administrative burden and the direct and more effective service of citizens and businesses. Among other things, the licensing and control framework for the manufacture of land antennas is reformed. The National Simplification Programme for the reduction of bureaucracy is set up. A Writing Observatory is also recommended for the duplication of administrative burdens arising from management’s legislation and regulatory acts. It is intended to establish a Single Digital Portal for Public Administration to help provide access to information, procedures and assistance and problem-solving services. The citizen and business interact quickly, easily and efficiently with the state, overcoming the chronic strains of bureaucracy. The daily transactions of citizens and businesses are facilitated, regardless of their place of residence, by having access to a wide range of online procedures without requiring the physical presence. Greece is transformed administratively and digitally into a modern Western state with respect for its citizens. All of this, together with all the arrangements proposed for the redefining of the framework for economic activity, the creation of a Single Digital Charter, the National Infrastructure Register, create the conditions for attracting and implementing investments, as well as for modernising administrative procedures, the overall recovery of the national economy and thus the prosperity and progress of the entire Greek society. That is why we are all called upon not only to vote in favour of the bill, but also to contribute to all levels of its rapid, uninterrupted and comprehensive implementation. The state is changing, Greece is changing. We're preparing for a new course, free of ideologies and strangles of the past. We want a state of effective, modern, citizen-friendly and business-friendly, and the New Democracy Government proves in every bill that it is ready to lead the country to a new era, era of growth, prosperity, innovation, always in the public interest and respect for man and the environment. Thank you.
